People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi. Thanks. Um, what brings me in... I guess it’s these thoughts I keep getting. I manage IT systems, and I keep getting stuck on this idea that if I miss one setting, like, just one access rule or a warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose absolutely everything. It hits me so hard, like I’m personally responsible. 

Which, actually, reminds me of this guy Greg I used to work with back in, what, 2012? Greg was always messing up the permissions on the shared drives. One time he accidentally gave the entire marketing department full admin rights to the executive financial servers! Can you imagine? We spent three weeks cleaning up that mess. I actually had to miss my nephew's birthday because of it. They had this whole pirate-themed party, rented a bouncy castle and everything, and I was stuck in a server room eating stale pretzels. My wife still brings that up, actually. She says I complain about Greg too much considering he doesn't even work in tech anymore, I think he opened a bakery or something? But it just goes to show how fast things fall apart, you know? 

But anyway... what was I saying? Right, the thoughts. Even though part of me knows it’s irrational to think every little thing is going to end in disaster... I just can’t move on. I can't let it go.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. I definitely don't invite them in. Like I was saying a minute ago, they just hit me out of nowhere—this sudden, intense flash that I missed one warning sign or some obscure access rule, and now someone is going to get hurt or lose absolutely everything because of me. It's completely unwanted. 

It actually reminds me of... you know those old malware pop-ups from the early 2000s? Like BonziBuddy, that little purple ape? Man, my uncle downloaded that on his old desktop and it completely destroyed his system registry. He actually called me at two in the morning on a Tuesday asking how to fix it. I was in grad school at the time, cramming for a huge network architecture final, running on nothing but espresso and sheer panic. I think I ended up having to completely wipe his hard drive over the phone while trying to memorize routing protocols. He lost all his bowling league spreadsheets, which he was furious about, but I told him, you know, you can't just click every link you see on the internet. 

But, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts popping into my head. Yeah, they're exactly like that. Just totally unprompted, malicious little pop-ups. And even though my logical brain knows it’s irrational to think that way, the feeling that I'm personally responsible just locks in, and I can't just move on from it. I just get stuck.

Yeah, they really are. It's getting bad. Like, at work... I actually missed this massive cloud migration deadline recently. I just couldn't bring myself to sign off on the final decisions. I kept thinking I missed a security group rule or something. And now my team is just... they're working around me. They don't even ask me for approvals anymore, they just go to the director. 

Which, honestly, my director is a whole other story. His name is Dave, and Dave still uses an AOL email address for his personal stuff. I saw it over his shoulder once during a screen share. AOL! In this day and age! I tried to explain to him why that's a terrible idea, especially with all the data breaches lately. Remember that huge Yahoo breach a few years ago? My wife's account got compromised in that one. Someone used it to sign up for, like, fifty different subscription boxes. We were getting artisanal cheeses and weird Japanese snacks delivered for months before we finally got it sorted out. The cheese was actually pretty good, though. We had this gouda that was... 

But anyway, yeah, the job. It's affecting the job. And my personal life, too. I used to play pickup basketball on Thursdays, and my wife and I did salsa dancing on Tuesday nights. I haven't gone to either in weeks. I just can't focus. I'm losing all these routines and the people that used to keep me grounded, you know? I'm just sitting at home, worrying about access logs while everyone else is out living their lives.

I’ve also stopped showing up for basketball and salsa nights, so I’m losing the routines and people that used to keep me grounded.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S065\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi. Thanks. Um, what brings me in... I guess it’s these thoughts I keep getting. I manage IT systems, and I keep getting stuck on this idea that if I miss one setting, like, just one access rule or a warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose absolutely everything. It hits me so hard, like I’m personally responsible. \n\nWhich, actually, reminds me of this guy Greg I used to work with back in, what, 2012? Greg was always messing up the permissions on the shared drives. One time he accidentally gave the entire marketing department full admin rights to the executive financial servers! Can you imagine? We spent three weeks cleaning up that mess. I actually had to miss my nephew's birthday because of it. They had this whole pirate-themed party, rented a bouncy castle and everything, and I was stuck in a server room eating stale pretzels. My wife still brings that up, actually. She says I complain about Greg too much considering he doesn't even work in tech anymore, I think he opened a bakery or something? But it just goes to show how fast things fall apart, you know? \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Right, the thoughts. Even though part of me knows it’s irrational to think every little thing is going to end in disaster... I just can’t move on. I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these thoughts about missing a setting and someone getting hurt are unwanted and pop into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I definitely don't invite them in. Like I was saying a minute ago, they just hit me out of nowhere—this sudden, intense flash that I missed one warning sign or some obscure access rule, and now someone is going to get hurt or lose absolutely everything because of me. It's completely unwanted. \n\nIt actually reminds me of... you know those old malware pop-ups from the early 2000s? Like BonziBuddy, that little purple ape? Man, my uncle downloaded that on his old desktop and it completely destroyed his system registry. He actually called me at two in the morning on a Tuesday asking how to fix it. I was in grad school at the time, cramming for a huge network architecture final, running on nothing but espresso and sheer panic. I think I ended up having to completely wipe his hard drive over the phone while trying to memorize routing protocols. He lost all his bowling league spreadsheets, which he was furious about, but I told him, you know, you can't just click every link you see on the internet. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts popping into my head. Yeah, they're exactly like that. Just totally unprompted, malicious little pop-ups. And even though my logical brain knows it’s irrational to think that way, the feeling that I'm personally responsible just locks in, and I can't just move on from it. I just get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting your ability to do your job or interact with your family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they really are. It's getting bad. Like, at work... I actually missed this massive cloud migration deadline recently. I just couldn't bring myself to sign off on the final decisions. I kept thinking I missed a security group rule or something. And now my team is just... they're working around me. They don't even ask me for approvals anymore, they just go to the director. \n\nWhich, honestly, my director is a whole other story. His name is Dave, and Dave still uses an AOL email address for his personal stuff. I saw it over his shoulder once during a screen share. AOL! In this day and age! I tried to explain to him why that's a terrible idea, especially with all the data breaches lately. Remember that huge Yahoo breach a few years ago? My wife's account got compromised in that one. Someone used it to sign up for, like, fifty different subscription boxes. We were getting artisanal cheeses and weird Japanese snacks delivered for months before we finally got it sorted out. The cheese was actually pretty good, though. We had this gouda that was... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the job. It's affecting the job. And my personal life, too. I used to play pickup basketball on Thursdays, and my wife and I did salsa dancing on Tuesday nights. I haven't gone to either in weeks. I just can't focus. I'm losing all these routines and the people that used to keep me grounded, you know? I'm just sitting at home, worrying about access logs while everyone else is out living their lives.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Values? No, no, not at all. That's the thing that really gets to me. I'm actually a very careful, conscientious person. I care about my team, I care about doing things right. I would never want anyone to get hurt or lose their data. That's why I'm in IT in the first place, to build systems that protect people. 

It's kind of like... well, my dad was a structural engineer. He designed bridges, mostly. He used to take me to his sites when I was a kid, make me wear this hard hat that was way too big for me. It kept slipping over my eyes. Anyway, he always talked about safety factors, how you build in redundancies so that if one thing fails, the whole structure doesn't collapse. He was obsessed with it. I think he actually drove my mom crazy because he'd apply that logic to everything. Like, he bought three different kinds of smoke detectors for the house just in case one brand had a manufacturing defect. We had alarms going off every time someone burned toast. I remember one Thanksgiving, the turkey got a little too crispy on the outside, and suddenly it sounded like a nuclear meltdown in our kitchen. We ended up eating dinner out on the patio in the freezing cold while the house aired out. 

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, my values. Yeah, so, these thoughts... they're completely against who I am. I want to build things that work, that are safe, like my dad did. But these thoughts make me feel like I'm a danger to everyone, like I'm this reckless person who's just waiting to ruin lives. And I know I'm not that guy, but the thoughts just... they don't care what I value. They just take over.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Alien. Definitely alien. They don't feel like me at all. It's like... well, speaking of alien, we have this one legacy server at work, this ancient IBM mainframe that we literally call the Mothership. It's been running since, I don't know, 1998? It sits in the basement of the old annex building and makes this rhythmic, pulsing hum that sounds exactly like the spaceship from Close Encounters. 

My buddy Greg in networking swears it's haunted. Greg’s a real character, actually. He collects vintage metal lunchboxes. He brought a Six Million Dollar Man one to the breakroom last week and absolutely refused to put it in the fridge because he said the condensation would ruin the vintage lithography. So we had to smell his egg salad sandwich sitting at room temperature all morning. It was brutal. I told him he was creating a literal biological hazard, which, ironically... 

Wait, sorry. I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Right, if the thoughts feel alien. Yeah. They do. Because, like I was saying, what brings me in is that I just keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, just one access rule or one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose absolutely everything. And even though a logical part of me knows it’s completely irrational, it just invades my head. It hits me like I’m personally responsible for the impending disaster, and I just can’t move on. It's like a hostile takeover of my own brain by something that isn't me.

Wait, didn't we just talk about my values? Beliefs and desires... I mean, no, definitely not. My only desire right now is to maybe get a decent night's sleep and finally finish building that mechanical keyboard I started working on.

Yeah... yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. I’ll reread our deployment plans, the audit logs, even check the door locks and old Slack messages over and over again. 

Speaking of the door locks, actually, our building management just installed these new biometric scanners on the main entrances. They’re supposed to be state-of-the-art, but the false rejection rate is just ridiculous. I was looking at the specs for their optical sensors, and they completely cheaped out on the glass. It gets smudged if you even breathe on it. The night security guard, Stan, has to wipe it down with a microfiber cloth like every hour. Stan’s a great guy, though. He brings in these incredible homemade tamales his wife makes. Pork with green chile, wrapped in actual corn husks, not that frozen stuff you get at the grocery store. 

Anyway, I usually end up eating them with him in the lobby because I’m always at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. I just... I can't leave. I have to stay and keep counting through the steps of my checks in my head until everything finally feels "safe." If I try to just pack up my bag and stop before it feels complete, I start to panic. Like, literal chest-tightening panic. So I just stay until the counting is done.

No, no, not at all. That's the thing that really gets to me. I'm actually a very careful, conscientious person. I care about my team, I care about doing things right. They’re supposed to be state-of-the-art, but the false rejection rate is just ridiculous. I was looking at the specs for their optical sensors, and they completely cheaped out on the glass. It gets smudged if you even breathe on it. The night security guard, Stan, has to wipe it down with a microfiber cloth like every hour. Stan’s a great guy, though. He brings in these incredible homemade tamales his wife makes. Pork with green chile, wrapped in actual corn husks, not that frozen stuff you get at the grocery store. \n\nAnyway, I usually end up eating them with him in the lobby because I’m always at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. I just... I can't leave. I have to stay and keep counting through the steps of my checks in my head until everything finally feels \"safe.\" If I try to just pack up my bag and stop before it feels complete, I start to panic. Like, literal chest-tightening panic. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. It's all about stopping the disaster. Like I was just saying, I'll reread the deployment plans, the audit logs, even old messages over and over, and I'm counting through those steps in my head until they finally feel "safe." Because if I don't... man, data loss is no joke. 

Actually, speaking of data, we’re doing a massive cloud migration right now and the AWS pricing structure is incredibly convoluted. My boss, David, is constantly breathing down my neck about the budget. Which is rich, considering he drives this immaculate vintage Porsche 911 to the office. He complains about a fifty-dollar server overage but probably spends ten grand a year just on imported spark plugs. I watched him try to parallel park it last Tuesday, and it literally took him six attempts. He was absolutely terrified of letting his tires touch the curb. It was painful to watch. 

But, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we saying? Right, preventing bad things. Yeah, that's exactly why I stay at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to stop checking the door locks or the logs before it feels complete, the panic just completely takes over. I have to do it to make sure nobody gets hurt and nothing gets lost.

Yeah, yeah, getting rid of the thoughts is the whole point. Well, that and making sure the disaster doesn't actually happen. It's like my brain won't shut up about it until I do the checks. But anyway, yeah, the thoughts. If I don't do the checks, the thoughts just get louder and louder. If I try to stop before it feels complete, the panic is just... it's unbearable. I have to finish the cycle to get the thoughts to stop.

We spent three weeks cleaning up that mess. I actually had to miss my nephew's birthday because of it. They had this whole pirate-themed party, rented a bouncy castle and everything, and I was stuck in a server room eating stale pretzels. My wife still brings that up, actually. She says I complain about Greg too much considering he doesn't even work in tech anymore, I think he opened a bakery or something? But it just goes to show how fast things fall apart, you know? \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Right, the thoughts. Even though part of me knows it’s irrational to think every little thing is going to end in disaster... I just can’t move on. I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these thoughts about missing a setting and someone getting hurt are unwanted and pop into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I definitely don't invite them in. Like I was saying a minute ago, they just hit me out of nowhere—this sudden, intense flash that I missed one warning sign or some obscure access rule, and now someone is going to get hurt or lose absolutely everything because of me. It's completely unwanted. \n\nIt actually reminds me of... you know those old malware pop-ups from the early 2000s? Like BonziBuddy, that little purple ape? Man, my uncle downloaded that on his old desktop and it completely destroyed his system registry. He actually called me at two in the morning on a Tuesday asking how to fix it. I was in grad school at the time, cramming for a huge network architecture final, running on nothing but espresso and sheer panic. I think I ended up having to completely wipe his hard drive over the phone while trying to memorize routing protocols. He lost all his bowling league spreadsheets, which he was furious about, but I told him, you know, you can't just click every link you see on the internet. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts popping into my head. Yeah, they're exactly like that. Just totally unprompted, malicious little pop-ups. And even though my logical brain knows it’s irrational to think that way, the feeling that I'm personally responsible just locks in, and I can't just move on from it. I just get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting your ability to do your job or interact with your family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they really are. It's getting bad. Like, at work... I actually missed this massive cloud migration deadline recently. I just couldn't bring myself to sign off on the final decisions. I kept thinking I missed a security group rule or something. And now my team is just... they're working around me. They don't even ask me for approvals anymore, they just go to the director. \n\nWhich, honestly, my director is a whole other story. His name is Dave, and Dave still uses an AOL email address for his personal stuff. I saw it over his shoulder once during a screen share. AOL! In this day and age! I tried to explain to him why that's a terrible idea, especially with all the data breaches lately. Remember that huge Yahoo breach a few years ago? My wife's account got compromised in that one. Someone used it to sign up for, like, fifty different subscription boxes. We were getting artisanal cheeses and weird Japanese snacks delivered for months before we finally got it sorted out. The cheese was actually pretty good, though. We had this gouda that was... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the job. It's affecting the job. And my personal life, too. I used to play pickup basketball on Thursdays, and my wife and I did salsa dancing on Tuesday nights. I haven't gone to either in weeks. I just can't focus. I'm losing all these routines and the people that used to keep me grounded, you know? I'm just sitting at home, worrying about access logs while everyone else is out living their lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: Values? No, no, not at all. That's the thing that really gets to me. I'm actually a very careful, conscientious person. I care about my team, I care about doing things right. I would never want anyone to get hurt or lose their data. That's why I'm in IT in the first place, to build systems that protect people. \n\nIt's kind of like... well, my dad was a structural engineer. He designed bridges, mostly. He used to take me to his sites when I was a kid, make me wear this hard hat that was way too big for me. It kept slipping over my eyes. Anyway, he always talked about safety factors, how you build in redundancies so that if one thing fails, the whole structure doesn't collapse. He was obsessed with it. I think he actually drove my mom crazy because he'd apply that logic to everything. Like, he bought three different kinds of smoke detectors for the house just in case one brand had a manufacturing defect. We had alarms going off every time someone burned toast. I remember one Thanksgiving, the turkey got a little too crispy on the outside, and suddenly it sounded like a nuclear meltdown in our kitchen. We ended up eating dinner out on the patio in the freezing cold while the house aired out. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, my values. Yeah, so, these thoughts... they're completely against who I am. I want to build things that work, that are safe, like my dad did. But these thoughts make me feel like I'm a danger to everyone, like I'm this reckless person who's just waiting to ruin lives. And I know I'm not that guy, but the thoughts just... they don't care what I value. They just take over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are a part of you or are they something that feels alien to you?\n\nPatient: Alien. Definitely alien. They don't feel like me at all. It's like... well, speaking of alien, we have this one legacy server at work, this ancient IBM mainframe that we literally call the Mothership. It's been running since, I don't know, 1998? It sits in the basement of the old annex building and makes this rhythmic, pulsing hum that sounds exactly like the spaceship from Close Encounters. \n\nMy buddy Greg in networking swears it's haunted. Greg’s a real character, actually. He collects vintage metal lunchboxes. He brought a Six Million Dollar Man one to the breakroom last week and absolutely refused to put it in the fridge because he said the condensation would ruin the vintage lithography. So we had to smell his egg salad sandwich sitting at room temperature all morning. It was brutal. I told him he was creating a literal biological hazard, which, ironically... \n\nWait, sorry. I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Right, if the thoughts feel alien. Yeah. They do. Because, like I was saying, what brings me in is that I just keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, just one access rule or one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose absolutely everything. And even though a logical part of me knows it’s completely irrational, it just invades my head. It hits me like I’m personally responsible for the impending disaster, and I just can’t move on. It's like a hostile takeover of my own brain by something that isn't me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are consistent with your own beliefs and desires?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't we just talk about my values? Beliefs and desires... I mean, no, definitely not. My only desire right now is to maybe get a decent night's sleep and finally finish building that mechanical keyboard I started working on. \n\nHave you ever looked into custom mechanical keyboards? It's a whole rabbit hole. You have to solder the switches yourself if you want a really specific feel. I bought these tactile switches because they have a nice little bump when you press them without being obnoxiously loud. My coworker, Sarah, uses these clicky blue switches, and I swear, it sounds like a hailstorm on a tin roof every time she types an email. I actually had to buy noise-canceling headphones just to sit in the same pod as her. I got the Sony ones, highly recommend them by the way. They block out absolutely everything. Sometimes I just wear them without any music playing just to hear myself think. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, I don't desire any of this. I think I just said that, didn't I? They're totally against what I want or believe. I just want to do my job and be normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or checks to try and alleviate these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. I’ll reread our deployment plans, the audit logs, even check the door locks and old Slack messages over and over again. \n\nSpeaking of the door locks, actually, our building management just installed these new biometric scanners on the main entrances. They’re supposed to be state-of-the-art, but the false rejection rate is just ridiculous. I was looking at the specs for their optical sensors, and they completely cheaped out on the glass. It gets smudged if you even breathe on it. The night security guard, Stan, has to wipe it down with a microfiber cloth like every hour. Stan’s a great guy, though. He brings in these incredible homemade tamales his wife makes. Pork with green chile, wrapped in actual corn husks, not that frozen stuff you get at the grocery store. \n\nAnyway, I usually end up eating them with him in the lobby because I’m always at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. I just... I can't leave. I have to stay and keep counting through the steps of my checks in my head until everything finally feels \"safe.\" If I try to just pack up my bag and stop before it feels complete, I start to panic. Like, literal chest-tightening panic. My boss, David, is constantly breathing down my neck about the budget. Which is rich, considering he drives this immaculate vintage Porsche 911 to the office. He complains about a fifty-dollar server overage but probably spends ten grand a year just on imported spark plugs. I watched him try to parallel park it last Tuesday, and it literally took him six attempts. He was absolutely terrified of letting his tires touch the curb. It was painful to watch. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we saying? Right, preventing bad things. Yeah, that's exactly why I stay at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. I have to do it to make sure nobody gets hurt and nothing gets lost.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these checks, are you trying to get rid of the thoughts about something going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, getting rid of the thoughts is the whole point. Yeah, for a minute. The anxiety drops right after I finish the whole cycle. Like I was saying, I'll reread the deployment plans, comb through the audit logs, check the door locks, and scroll through those old messages over and over. And I'm counting through the steps in my head the entire time. Once I hit that point where it finally feels "safe," the relief is huge. 

It’s kind of like the feeling you get when you finally figure out a bug in the code that’s been driving you crazy for days. We had this one issue last month with our API rate limiting—it was just randomly dropping requests. Turns out, it was a timezone configuration error on one of the legacy servers. The guy who originally set it up, Mark, retired like three years ago. He moved to Florida and bought this massive pontoon boat. He posts pictures of it on Facebook all the time. He named it "The Salty Byte," which I guess is kind of clever for an IT guy. He always used to wear these really loud Hawaiian shirts to the office on Fridays. 

But anyway, yeah, the relief. It’s there, but it doesn't last. The anxiety always creeps back. But I have to get that temporary relief, which is why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to pack up and stop before the counting and checking feel completely finished, the panic just hits me like a truck. I have to finish it to get that drop in anxiety, even if it’s just for a little while.

Greg was always messing up the permissions on the shared drives. One time he accidentally gave the entire marketing department full admin rights to the executive financial servers! Can you imagine? We spent three weeks cleaning up that mess. I actually had to miss my nephew's birthday because of it. They had this whole pirate-themed party, rented a bouncy castle and everything, and I was stuck in a server room eating stale pretzels. My wife still brings that up, actually. She says I complain about Greg too much considering he doesn't even work in tech anymore, I think he opened a bakery or something? But it just goes to show how fast things fall apart, you know? \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Right, the thoughts. Even though part of me knows it’s irrational to think every little thing is going to end in disaster... I just can’t move on. I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these thoughts about missing a setting and someone getting hurt are unwanted and pop into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I definitely don't invite them in. Like I was saying a minute ago, they just hit me out of nowhere—this sudden, intense flash that I missed one warning sign or some obscure access rule, and now someone is going to get hurt or lose absolutely everything because of me. It's completely unwanted. \n\nIt actually reminds me of... you know those old malware pop-ups from the early 2000s? Like BonziBuddy, that little purple ape? Man, my uncle downloaded that on his old desktop and it completely destroyed his system registry. He actually called me at two in the morning on a Tuesday asking how to fix it. I was in grad school at the time, cramming for a huge network architecture final, running on nothing but espresso and sheer panic. I think I ended up having to completely wipe his hard drive over the phone while trying to memorize routing protocols. He lost all his bowling league spreadsheets, which he was furious about, but I told him, you know, you can't just click every link you see on the internet. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the thoughts popping into my head. Yeah, they're exactly like that. Just totally unprompted, malicious little pop-ups. And even though my logical brain knows it’s irrational to think that way, the feeling that I'm personally responsible just locks in, and I can't just move on from it. I just get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts affecting your ability to do your job or interact with your family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they really are. It's getting bad. Like, at work... I actually missed this massive cloud migration deadline recently. I just couldn't bring myself to sign off on the final decisions. I kept thinking I missed a security group rule or something. And now my team is just... they're working around me. They don't even ask me for approvals anymore, they just go to the director. \n\nWhich, honestly, my director is a whole other story. His name is Dave, and Dave still uses an AOL email address for his personal stuff. I saw it over his shoulder once during a screen share. AOL! In this day and age! I tried to explain to him why that's a terrible idea, especially with all the data breaches lately. Remember that huge Yahoo breach a few years ago? My wife's account got compromised in that one. Someone used it to sign up for, like, fifty different subscription boxes. We were getting artisanal cheeses and weird Japanese snacks delivered for months before we finally got it sorted out. The cheese was actually pretty good, though. We had this gouda that was... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the job. It's affecting the job. And my personal life, too. I used to play pickup basketball on Thursdays, and my wife and I did salsa dancing on Tuesday nights. I haven't gone to either in weeks. I just can't focus. I'm losing all these routines and the people that used to keep me grounded, you know? I'm just sitting at home, worrying about access logs while everyone else is out living their lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: Values? No, no, not at all. That's the thing that really gets to me. I'm actually a very careful, conscientious person. I care about my team, I care about doing things right. I would never want anyone to get hurt or lose their data. That's why I'm in IT in the first place, to build systems that protect people. \n\nIt's kind of like... well, my dad was a structural engineer. He designed bridges, mostly. He used to take me to his sites when I was a kid, make me wear this hard hat that was way too big for me. It kept slipping over my eyes. Anyway, he always talked about safety factors, how you build in redundancies so that if one thing fails, the whole structure doesn't collapse. He was obsessed with it. I think he actually drove my mom crazy because he'd apply that logic to everything. Like, he bought three different kinds of smoke detectors for the house just in case one brand had a manufacturing defect. We had alarms going off every time someone burned toast. I remember one Thanksgiving, the turkey got a little too crispy on the outside, and suddenly it sounded like a nuclear meltdown in our kitchen. We ended up eating dinner out on the patio in the freezing cold while the house aired out. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, my values. Yeah, so, these thoughts... they're completely against who I am. I want to build things that work, that are safe, like my dad did. But these thoughts make me feel like I'm a danger to everyone, like I'm this reckless person who's just waiting to ruin lives. And I know I'm not that guy, but the thoughts just... they don't care what I value. They just take over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are a part of you or are they something that feels alien to you?\n\nPatient: Alien. Definitely alien. They don't feel like me at all. It's like... well, speaking of alien, we have this one legacy server at work, this ancient IBM mainframe that we literally call the Mothership. It's been running since, I don't know, 1998? It sits in the basement of the old annex building and makes this rhythmic, pulsing hum that sounds exactly like the spaceship from Close Encounters. \n\nMy buddy Greg in networking swears it's haunted. Greg’s a real character, actually. He collects vintage metal lunchboxes. He brought a Six Million Dollar Man one to the breakroom last week and absolutely refused to put it in the fridge because he said the condensation would ruin the vintage lithography. So we had to smell his egg salad sandwich sitting at room temperature all morning. It was brutal. I told him he was creating a literal biological hazard, which, ironically... \n\nWait, sorry. I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Right, if the thoughts feel alien. Yeah. They do. Because, like I was saying, what brings me in is that I just keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, just one access rule or one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose absolutely everything. And even though a logical part of me knows it’s completely irrational, it just invades my head. It hits me like I’m personally responsible for the impending disaster, and I just can’t move on. It's like a hostile takeover of my own brain by something that isn't me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are consistent with your own beliefs and desires?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't we just talk about my values? Beliefs and desires... I mean, no, definitely not. My only desire right now is to maybe get a decent night's sleep and finally finish building that mechanical keyboard I started working on. \n\nHave you ever looked into custom mechanical keyboards? It's a whole rabbit hole. You have to solder the switches yourself if you want a really specific feel. I bought these tactile switches because they have a nice little bump when you press them without being obnoxiously loud. My coworker, Sarah, uses these clicky blue switches, and I swear, it sounds like a hailstorm on a tin roof every time she types an email. I actually had to buy noise-canceling headphones just to sit in the same pod as her. I got the Sony ones, highly recommend them by the way. They block out absolutely everything. Sometimes I just wear them without any music playing just to hear myself think. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, I don't desire any of this. I think I just said that, didn't I? They're totally against what I want or believe. I just want to do my job and be normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or checks to try and alleviate these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. I’ll reread our deployment plans, the audit logs, even check the door locks and old Slack messages over and over again. \n\nSpeaking of the door locks, actually, our building management just installed these new biometric scanners on the main entrances. They’re supposed to be state-of-the-art, but the false rejection rate is just ridiculous. I was looking at the specs for their optical sensors, and they completely cheaped out on the glass. It gets smudged if you even breathe on it. The night security guard, Stan, has to wipe it down with a microfiber cloth like every hour. Stan’s a great guy, though. He brings in these incredible homemade tamales his wife makes. Pork with green chile, wrapped in actual corn husks, not that frozen stuff you get at the grocery store. \n\nAnyway, I usually end up eating them with him in the lobby because I’m always at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. I just... I can't leave. I have to stay and keep counting through the steps of my checks in my head until everything finally feels \"safe.\" If I try to just pack up my bag and stop before it feels complete, I start to panic. Like, literal chest-tightening panic. So I just stay until the counting is done.\n\nDoctor: When you feel compelled to reread deployment plans or check the door locks, is it specifically to prevent someone from getting hurt or data being lost?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's all about stopping the disaster. Like I was just saying, I'll reread the deployment plans, the audit logs, even old messages over and over, and I'm counting through those steps in my head until they finally feel \"safe.\" Because if I don't... man, data loss is no joke. \n\nActually, speaking of data, we’re doing a massive cloud migration right now and the AWS pricing structure is incredibly convoluted. My boss, David, is constantly breathing down my neck about the budget. Which is rich, considering he drives this immaculate vintage Porsche 911 to the office. He complains about a fifty-dollar server overage but probably spends ten grand a year just on imported spark plugs. I watched him try to parallel park it last Tuesday, and it literally took him six attempts. He was absolutely terrified of letting his tires touch the curb. It was painful to watch. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we saying? Right, preventing bad things. Yeah, that's exactly why I stay at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to stop checking the door locks or the logs before it feels complete, the panic just completely takes over. I have to do it to make sure nobody gets hurt and nothing gets lost.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these checks, are you trying to get rid of the thoughts about something going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, getting rid of the thoughts is the whole point. Well, that and making sure the disaster doesn't actually happen. It's like my brain won't shut up about it until I do the checks. So, like I was saying before, I'll reread the deployment plans, check the audit logs, test the door locks... oh, and the old messages. I have to read those over and over, too. And I'm counting the steps in my head the whole time.\n\nSpeaking of counting, did I tell you about my step-tracker? I got one of those Garmin watches a few months ago because my doctor said I needed more cardio. The interface is actually really slick, but the GPS gets completely confused if I walk between tall buildings downtown. It'll say I walked three miles when I just went around the block to get coffee. There's this little place on 4th Street that does pour-overs, and the barista—he's got this massive handlebar mustache, looks like he stepped out of 1890—he takes exactly four minutes to make one cup. It's an art form, really. But the line is always out the door, so you have to budget like twenty minutes just to get a coffee.\n\nBut anyway, yeah, the thoughts. If I don't do the checks, the thoughts just get louder and louder. I have to keep checking the logs and the locks and counting until it finally clicks and feels \"safe.\" That's why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else is gone. If I try to stop before it feels complete, the panic is just... it's unbearable. I have to finish the cycle to get the thoughts to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after completing these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, for a minute. The anxiety drops right after I finish the whole cycle. Like I was saying, I'll reread the deployment plans, comb through the audit logs, check the door locks, and scroll through those old messages over and over. And I'm counting through the steps in my head the entire time. Once I hit that point where it finally feels \"safe,\" the relief is huge. \n\nIt’s kind of like the feeling you get when you finally figure out a bug in the code that’s been driving you crazy for days. We had this one issue last month with our API rate limiting—it was just randomly dropping requests. Turns out, it was a timezone configuration error on one of the legacy servers. The guy who originally set it up, Mark, retired like three years ago. He moved to Florida and bought this massive pontoon boat. He posts pictures of it on Facebook all the time. He named it \"The Salty Byte,\" which I guess is kind of clever for an IT guy. He always used to wear these really loud Hawaiian shirts to the office on Fridays. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the relief. It’s there, but it doesn't last. The anxiety always creeps back. But I have to get that temporary relief, which is why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to pack up and stop before the counting and checking feel completely finished, the panic just hits me like a truck. I have to finish it to get that drop in anxiety, even if it’s just for a little while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F42", "F41.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

If I try to pack up and stop before the counting and checking feel completely finished, the panic just hits me like a truck. Yeah, the counting is a big part of it. It's not always the exact same number every time, but I have to keep counting through the steps in my head while I do the checks until it hits that specific "safe" feeling. So I'm rereading the deployment plans, checking the audit logs, rattling the door locks, scrolling the old messages... and I'm counting the whole time. 

It's exhausting, honestly. Kind of like when I was trying to teach my nephew how to play chess last weekend. He's eight, and he has zero attention span. We were using this beautiful wooden chess set that my grandfather carved by hand back in the sixties. He used to sit on his porch in Vermont and just whittle away for hours. He made all the knights look like actual horses he owned on his farm. Anyway, my nephew kept trying to make the pawns move backwards, and every time I corrected him, he'd just knock the pieces over and laugh. Kids, right? I love him, but spending two hours trying to explain basic rules to a second-grader will drain the life right out of you. 

Wait, what was I... right, the repeating. Yeah, I have to keep repeating the checks and counting the steps until it feels complete. If I don't hit that "safe" feeling, the panic is overwhelming. That's exactly why I'm stuck at the office for hours after everyone else goes home. I just can't stop until it's done right.

He named it \"The Salty Byte,\" which I guess is kind of clever for an IT guy. He always used to wear these really loud Hawaiian shirts to the office on Fridays. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the relief. It’s there, but it doesn't last. The anxiety always creeps back. But I have to get that temporary relief, which is why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to pack up and stop before the counting and checking feel completely finished, the panic just hits me like a truck. I have to finish it to get that drop in anxiety, even if it’s just for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checks a specific number of times to feel like you've done them correctly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the counting is a big part of it. It's not always the exact same number every time, but I have to keep counting through the steps in my head while I do the checks until it hits that specific \"safe\" feeling. So I'm rereading the deployment plans, checking the audit logs, rattling the door locks, scrolling the old messages... and I'm counting the whole time. \n\nIt's exhausting, honestly. Kind of like when I was trying to teach my nephew how to play chess last weekend. He's eight, and he has zero attention span. We were using this beautiful wooden chess set that my grandfather carved by hand back in the sixties. He used to sit on his porch in Vermont and just whittle away for hours. He made all the knights look like actual horses he owned on his farm. Anyway, my nephew kept trying to make the pawns move backwards, and every time I corrected him, he'd just knock the pieces over and laugh. Kids, right? I love him, but spending two hours trying to explain basic rules to a second-grader will drain the life right out of you. \n\nWait, what was I... right, the repeating. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. It’s all about preventing a massive disaster, like a catastrophic data wipe or someone breaking in and getting hurt. Losing data is... well, it's pretty much my worst nightmare. 

Speaking of losing data, my sister’s wedding photographer actually lost all of her reception photos a few years ago. He dropped his external hard drive right on the pavement. It was one of those older spinning disk drives, not a solid-state one, which I told him was a huge risk for field work. The wedding was at this gorgeous renovated barn up in upstate New York. They had these vintage Edison bulbs strung up everywhere across the rafters, and the food was incredible. They served these mini beef wellingtons during cocktail hour—I swear I must have eaten like fifteen of them. The caterer was this very intense French guy who kept yelling at his staff in the back. You could hear him all the way from the dance floor whenever the DJ paused the music. 

But anyway... yeah, a disaster. I can't let a disaster happen at work. So, I know I keep saying it, but that's exactly why I’ll just reread the deployment plans, comb through the audit logs, check the door locks, and review those old messages over and over again. I'm counting through the steps in my head the whole time until it finally hits that "safe" feeling. If I try to pack up my bag and stop before it feels completely resolved, the panic is just... it's completely overwhelming. I can't breathe. So I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves, just to make sure nothing terrible is going to happen.

Yeah, so, these thoughts... they're completely against who I am. I want to build things that work, that are safe, like my dad did. But these thoughts make me feel like I'm a danger to everyone, like I'm this reckless person who's just waiting to ruin lives. And I know I'm not that guy, but the thoughts just... they don't care what I value. They just take over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are a part of you or are they something that feels alien to you?\n\nPatient: Alien. Definitely alien. They don't feel like me at all. It's like... well, speaking of alien, we have this one legacy server at work, this ancient IBM mainframe that we literally call the Mothership. It's been running since, I don't know, 1998? It sits in the basement of the old annex building and makes this rhythmic, pulsing hum that sounds exactly like the spaceship from Close Encounters. \n\nMy buddy Greg in networking swears it's haunted. Greg’s a real character, actually. He collects vintage metal lunchboxes. He brought a Six Million Dollar Man one to the breakroom last week and absolutely refused to put it in the fridge because he said the condensation would ruin the vintage lithography. So we had to smell his egg salad sandwich sitting at room temperature all morning. It was brutal. I told him he was creating a literal biological hazard, which, ironically... \n\nWait, sorry. I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Right, if the thoughts feel alien. Yeah. They do. Because, like I was saying, what brings me in is that I just keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, just one access rule or one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose absolutely everything. And even though a logical part of me knows it’s completely irrational, it just invades my head. It hits me like I’m personally responsible for the impending disaster, and I just can’t move on. It's like a hostile takeover of my own brain by something that isn't me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these intrusive thoughts are consistent with your own beliefs and desires?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't we just talk about my values? Beliefs and desires... I mean, no, definitely not. My only desire right now is to maybe get a decent night's sleep and finally finish building that mechanical keyboard I started working on. \n\nHave you ever looked into custom mechanical keyboards? It's a whole rabbit hole. You have to solder the switches yourself if you want a really specific feel. I bought these tactile switches because they have a nice little bump when you press them without being obnoxiously loud. My coworker, Sarah, uses these clicky blue switches, and I swear, it sounds like a hailstorm on a tin roof every time she types an email. I actually had to buy noise-canceling headphones just to sit in the same pod as her. I got the Sony ones, highly recommend them by the way. They block out absolutely everything. Sometimes I just wear them without any music playing just to hear myself think. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, I don't desire any of this. I think I just said that, didn't I? They're totally against what I want or believe. I just want to do my job and be normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or checks to try and alleviate these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. I’ll reread our deployment plans, the audit logs, even check the door locks and old Slack messages over and over again. \n\nSpeaking of the door locks, actually, our building management just installed these new biometric scanners on the main entrances. They’re supposed to be state-of-the-art, but the false rejection rate is just ridiculous. I was looking at the specs for their optical sensors, and they completely cheaped out on the glass. It gets smudged if you even breathe on it. The night security guard, Stan, has to wipe it down with a microfiber cloth like every hour. Stan’s a great guy, though. He brings in these incredible homemade tamales his wife makes. Pork with green chile, wrapped in actual corn husks, not that frozen stuff you get at the grocery store. \n\nAnyway, I usually end up eating them with him in the lobby because I’m always at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. I just... I can't leave. I have to stay and keep counting through the steps of my checks in my head until everything finally feels \"safe.\" If I try to just pack up my bag and stop before it feels complete, I start to panic. Like, literal chest-tightening panic. So I just stay until the counting is done.\n\nDoctor: When you feel compelled to reread deployment plans or check the door locks, is it specifically to prevent someone from getting hurt or data being lost?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It's all about stopping the disaster. Like I was just saying, I'll reread the deployment plans, the audit logs, even old messages over and over, and I'm counting through those steps in my head until they finally feel \"safe.\" Because if I don't... man, data loss is no joke. \n\nActually, speaking of data, we’re doing a massive cloud migration right now and the AWS pricing structure is incredibly convoluted. My boss, David, is constantly breathing down my neck about the budget. Which is rich, considering he drives this immaculate vintage Porsche 911 to the office. He complains about a fifty-dollar server overage but probably spends ten grand a year just on imported spark plugs. I watched him try to parallel park it last Tuesday, and it literally took him six attempts. He was absolutely terrified of letting his tires touch the curb. It was painful to watch. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we saying? Right, preventing bad things. Yeah, that's exactly why I stay at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to stop checking the door locks or the logs before it feels complete, the panic just completely takes over. I have to do it to make sure nobody gets hurt and nothing gets lost.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these checks, are you trying to get rid of the thoughts about something going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, getting rid of the thoughts is the whole point. Well, that and making sure the disaster doesn't actually happen. It's like my brain won't shut up about it until I do the checks. So, like I was saying before, I'll reread the deployment plans, check the audit logs, test the door locks... oh, and the old messages. I have to read those over and over, too. And I'm counting the steps in my head the whole time.\n\nSpeaking of counting, did I tell you about my step-tracker? I got one of those Garmin watches a few months ago because my doctor said I needed more cardio. The interface is actually really slick, but the GPS gets completely confused if I walk between tall buildings downtown. It'll say I walked three miles when I just went around the block to get coffee. There's this little place on 4th Street that does pour-overs, and the barista—he's got this massive handlebar mustache, looks like he stepped out of 1890—he takes exactly four minutes to make one cup. It's an art form, really. But the line is always out the door, so you have to budget like twenty minutes just to get a coffee.\n\nBut anyway, yeah, the thoughts. If I don't do the checks, the thoughts just get louder and louder. I have to keep checking the logs and the locks and counting until it finally clicks and feels \"safe.\" That's why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else is gone. If I try to stop before it feels complete, the panic is just... it's unbearable. I have to finish the cycle to get the thoughts to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after completing these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, for a minute. The anxiety drops right after I finish the whole cycle. Like I was saying, I'll reread the deployment plans, comb through the audit logs, check the door locks, and scroll through those old messages over and over. And I'm counting through the steps in my head the entire time. Once I hit that point where it finally feels \"safe,\" the relief is huge. \n\nIt’s kind of like the feeling you get when you finally figure out a bug in the code that’s been driving you crazy for days. We had this one issue last month with our API rate limiting—it was just randomly dropping requests. Turns out, it was a timezone configuration error on one of the legacy servers. The guy who originally set it up, Mark, retired like three years ago. He moved to Florida and bought this massive pontoon boat. He posts pictures of it on Facebook all the time. He named it \"The Salty Byte,\" which I guess is kind of clever for an IT guy. He always used to wear these really loud Hawaiian shirts to the office on Fridays. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the relief. It’s there, but it doesn't last. The anxiety always creeps back. But I have to get that temporary relief, which is why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to pack up and stop before the counting and checking feel completely finished, the panic just hits me like a truck. I have to finish it to get that drop in anxiety, even if it’s just for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checks a specific number of times to feel like you've done them correctly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the counting is a big part of it. It's not always the exact same number every time, but I have to keep counting through the steps in my head while I do the checks until it hits that specific \"safe\" feeling. So I'm rereading the deployment plans, checking the audit logs, rattling the door locks, scrolling the old messages... and I'm counting the whole time. \n\nIt's exhausting, honestly. We were using this beautiful wooden chess set that my grandfather carved by hand back in the sixties. He used to sit on his porch in Vermont and just whittle away for hours. He made all the knights look like actual horses he owned on his farm. Anyway, my nephew kept trying to make the pawns move backwards, and every time I corrected him, he'd just knock the pieces over and laugh. Kids, right? I love him, but spending two hours trying to explain basic rules to a second-grader will drain the life right out of you. \n\nWait, what was I... right, the repeating. Yeah, I have to keep repeating the checks and counting the steps until it feels complete. If I don't hit that \"safe\" feeling, the panic is overwhelming. That's exactly why I'm stuck at the office for hours after everyone else goes home. I just can't stop until it's done right.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks and counting behaviors specifically intended to prevent someone from getting hurt or data being lost?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It’s all about preventing a massive disaster, like a catastrophic data wipe or someone breaking in and getting hurt. Losing data is... well, it's pretty much my worst nightmare. \n\nSpeaking of losing data, my sister’s wedding photographer actually lost all of her reception photos a few years ago. He dropped his external hard drive right on the pavement. It was one of those older spinning disk drives, not a solid-state one, which I told him was a huge risk for field work. The wedding was at this gorgeous renovated barn up in upstate New York. They had these vintage Edison bulbs strung up everywhere across the rafters, and the food was incredible. They served these mini beef wellingtons during cocktail hour—I swear I must have eaten like fifteen of them. The caterer was this very intense French guy who kept yelling at his staff in the back. Well, part of me knows it’s irrational. I mean, logically, I know that. But I just keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, you know, one access rule, one little warning sign... someone could get hurt or lose everything. And it hits me like I’m personally responsible for it. It’s this massive weight. 

Speaking of being responsible, it kind of reminds me of when I was helping my brother-in-law build his back deck last summer. He is absolutely terrible with tools—just zero spatial awareness. But he went out and bought this huge compound miter saw on sale at Home Depot. The guy working in the hardware section—he had this crazy handlebar mustache, looked exactly like he belonged in a barbershop quartet—he told us we needed a specific type of blade for the composite decking we were using. Well, my brother-in-law didn't listen, bought the cheap one, and the very first time he turned it on, it kicked back and nearly took his thumb off. We spent six hours in the ER that Saturday. The waiting room TV was stuck on this marathon of old 90s game shows. I used to love Supermarket Sweep when I was a kid. You remember that one? Running down the aisles throwing those giant inflatable hams into the cart... I always thought I'd be so good at that. 

But anyway... what were we talking about? Right, the feeling. Yeah, even though I know it's probably irrational, the feeling that it's all on my shoulders is just too real. I can’t just move on and ignore the thoughts, because if I do and something catastrophic actually happens, it's entirely my fault.
