My boss, David, is constantly breathing down my neck about the budget. Which is rich, considering he drives this immaculate vintage Porsche 911 to the office. He complains about a fifty-dollar server overage but probably spends ten grand a year just on imported spark plugs. I watched him try to parallel park it last Tuesday, and it literally took him six attempts. He was absolutely terrified of letting his tires touch the curb. It was painful to watch. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we saying? Right, preventing bad things. Yeah, that's exactly why I stay at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to stop checking the door locks or the logs before it feels complete, the panic just completely takes over. I have to do it to make sure nobody gets hurt and nothing gets lost.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these checks, are you trying to get rid of the thoughts about something going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, getting rid of the thoughts is the whole point. Well, that and making sure the disaster doesn't actually happen. It's like my brain won't shut up about it until I do the checks. So, like I was saying before, I'll reread the deployment plans, check the audit logs, test the door locks... oh, and the old messages. I have to read those over and over, too. And I'm counting the steps in my head the whole time.\n\nSpeaking of counting, did I tell you about my step-tracker? I got one of those Garmin watches a few months ago because my doctor said I needed more cardio. The interface is actually really slick, but the GPS gets completely confused if I walk between tall buildings downtown. It'll say I walked three miles when I just went around the block to get coffee. There's this little place on 4th Street that does pour-overs, and the barista—he's got this massive handlebar mustache, looks like he stepped out of 1890—he takes exactly four minutes to make one cup. It's an art form, really. But the line is always out the door, so you have to budget like twenty minutes just to get a coffee.\n\nBut anyway, yeah, the thoughts. If I don't do the checks, the thoughts just get louder and louder. I have to keep checking the logs and the locks and counting until it finally clicks and feels \"safe.\" That's why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else is gone. If I try to stop before it feels complete, the panic is just... it's unbearable. I have to finish the cycle to get the thoughts to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after completing these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, for a minute. The anxiety drops right after I finish the whole cycle. Like I was saying, I'll reread the deployment plans, comb through the audit logs, check the door locks, and scroll through those old messages over and over. And I'm counting through the steps in my head the entire time. Once I hit that point where it finally feels \"safe,\" the relief is huge. \n\nIt’s kind of like the feeling you get when you finally figure out a bug in the code that’s been driving you crazy for days. We had this one issue last month with our API rate limiting—it was just randomly dropping requests. Turns out, it was a timezone configuration error on one of the legacy servers. The guy who originally set it up, Mark, retired like three years ago. He moved to Florida and bought this massive pontoon boat. He posts pictures of it on Facebook all the time. He named it \"The Salty Byte,\" which I guess is kind of clever for an IT guy. He always used to wear these really loud Hawaiian shirts to the office on Fridays. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the relief. It’s there, but it doesn't last. The anxiety always creeps back. But I have to get that temporary relief, which is why I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves. If I try to pack up and stop before the counting and checking feel completely finished, the panic just hits me like a truck. I have to finish it to get that drop in anxiety, even if it’s just for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat these checks a specific number of times to feel like you've done them correctly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the counting is a big part of it. It's not always the exact same number every time, but I have to keep counting through the steps in my head while I do the checks until it hits that specific \"safe\" feeling. So I'm rereading the deployment plans, checking the audit logs, rattling the door locks, scrolling the old messages... and I'm counting the whole time. \n\nIt's exhausting, honestly. Kind of like when I was trying to teach my nephew how to play chess last weekend. He's eight, and he has zero attention span. We were using this beautiful wooden chess set that my grandfather carved by hand back in the sixties. He used to sit on his porch in Vermont and just whittle away for hours. He made all the knights look like actual horses he owned on his farm. Anyway, my nephew kept trying to make the pawns move backwards, and every time I corrected him, he'd just knock the pieces over and laugh. Kids, right? I love him, but spending two hours trying to explain basic rules to a second-grader will drain the life right out of you. \n\nWait, what was I... right, the repeating. Yeah, I have to keep repeating the checks and counting the steps until it feels complete. If I don't hit that \"safe\" feeling, the panic is overwhelming. That's exactly why I'm stuck at the office for hours after everyone else goes home. I just can't stop until it's done right.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks and counting behaviors specifically intended to prevent someone from getting hurt or data being lost?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. It’s all about preventing a massive disaster, like a catastrophic data wipe or someone breaking in and getting hurt. Losing data is... well, it's pretty much my worst nightmare. \n\nSpeaking of losing data, my sister’s wedding photographer actually lost all of her reception photos a few years ago. He dropped his external hard drive right on the pavement. It was one of those older spinning disk drives, not a solid-state one, which I told him was a huge risk for field work. The wedding was at this gorgeous renovated barn up in upstate New York. They had these vintage Edison bulbs strung up everywhere across the rafters, and the food was incredible. They served these mini beef wellingtons during cocktail hour—I swear I must have eaten like fifteen of them. The caterer was this very intense French guy who kept yelling at his staff in the back. You could hear him all the way from the dance floor whenever the DJ paused the music. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, a disaster. I can't let a disaster happen at work. So, I know I keep saying it, but that's exactly why I’ll just reread the deployment plans, comb through the audit logs, check the door locks, and review those old messages over and over again. I'm counting through the steps in my head the whole time until it finally hits that \"safe\" feeling. If I try to pack up my bag and stop before it feels completely resolved, the panic is just... it's completely overwhelming. I can't breathe. So I end up staying at the office for hours after everyone else leaves, just to make sure nothing terrible is going to happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something bad would really happen if you didn't complete these checks, or is it more about the feeling inside that you need to do them?\n\nPatient: Well, part of me knows it’s irrational. I mean, logically, I know that. But I just keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, you know, one access rule, one little warning sign... someone could get hurt or lose everything. And it hits me like I’m personally responsible for it. It’s this massive weight. \n\nSpeaking of being responsible, it kind of reminds me of when I was helping my brother-in-law build his back deck last summer. He is absolutely terrible with tools—just zero spatial awareness. But he went out and bought this huge compound miter saw on sale at Home Depot. The guy working in the hardware section—he had this crazy handlebar mustache, looked exactly like he belonged in a barbershop quartet—he told us we needed a specific type of blade for the composite decking we were using. Well, my brother-in-law didn't listen, bought the cheap one, and the very first time he turned it on, it kicked back and nearly took his thumb off. We spent six hours in the ER that Saturday. The waiting room TV was stuck on this marathon of old 90s game shows. I used to love Supermarket Sweep when I was a kid. You remember that one? Running down the aisles throwing those giant inflatable hams into the cart... I always thought I'd be so good at that. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Right, the feeling. Yeah, even though I know it's probably irrational, the feeling that it's all on my shoulders is just too real. I can’t just move on and ignore the thoughts, because if I do and something catastrophic actually happens, it's entirely my fault.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of something bad actually happening that drives you to do these checks, or is it more about making the anxious feeling inside you go away?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's really both, I guess. The thought of something terrible happening is what starts it, but the anxiety is what keeps me trapped. If I don't do the checks, the anxiety just builds and builds until I feel like I'm going to explode. \n\nThat anxiety feeling... it's kind of like being stuck in traffic on the George Washington Bridge when you really have to use the bathroom. Have you ever had that happen? I was driving back from New Jersey last month after helping my buddy move. He got this amazing mid-century modern couch off Facebook Marketplace, but it weighed a ton. We had to pivot it up three flights of stairs in this tiny pre-war walk-up. The hallway smelled like cabbage and old carpet. Anyway, I was driving back, and traffic just stopped dead. Dead stop for forty-five minutes. And I had drank this massive iced coffee right before leaving. The panic I felt sitting in that car, nowhere to go, just watching the brake lights... it was excruciating. I ended up having to pull off in Fort Lee and beg a gas station attendant to let me use their employee restroom. He was this really nice guy, actually, told me all about his daughter who was applying to colleges. She wanted to study marine biology, which I always thought was fascinating. I’ve actually completely stopped showing up for my regular weekend basketball games and my Thursday salsa nights. I used to love salsa. My instructor, Hector, he’s this incredibly energetic guy from Bogota, and he always wears these impossibly shiny black dance shoes. I actually tried to buy a pair just like them once. I ordered them online from this specialty shop in Italy, but they got held up in customs for like a month. When they finally arrived, the delivery driver practically threw the box at my porch. He was driving one of those new electric delivery vans—you know the ones that make that weird, high-pitched spaceship humming noise when they reverse? I was reading this huge article the other day about how those corporate fleets are having major battery drain issues in cold weather, which makes sense because lithium-ion cells really degrade when the temperature drops below freezing. My cousin bought an EV last winter and he was furious because his range dropped by like forty percent... \n\nBut, uh, sorry, what was I saying? Right, the salsa nights. Yeah, I just stopped going to all of it. I’m losing the routines and the people that used to keep me grounded, all because I'm stuck at the office doing my checks. And the worst part is, it's ruining my work life too, even though I'm spending all my time there. I recently missed a major cloud migration deadline because I just couldn’t finish signing off on the architectural decisions. I was too paralyzed. Now my team has just started working around me entirely instead of relying on me. So yeah... it's messing up pretty much everything.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your need to perform these checks is significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is. I mean, like I was saying, I've completely stopped showing up for my weekend basketball games, and my Thursday salsa nights are just... gone. I really miss it. It's like I'm losing all the routines and the people that used to keep me grounded. \n\nAnd you know, speaking of being grounded, I was trying to fix this issue with my espresso machine the other morning. I ended up using a wire coat hanger to try and fish it out, and I pulled out, like, three cat toys. And it's not even just the fun stuff, it's work, too. He had this massive garage, totally tricked out with custom tool chests and neon signs. He actually gave me this old neon Budweiser sign, which I hung up in my basement until the transformer blew and it started buzzing so loudly I couldn't hear the TV. I tried to fix the transformer myself, watching all these YouTube tutorials. There's this one channel, this guy in Australia who fixes old electronics, and he has the most soothing voice. I ended up falling asleep watching a video about repairing a 1980s Betamax player. My uncle had a Betamax player when I was growing up. We used to watch the original Star Wars on it, over and over...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The thoughts. Right. Yeah, so these thoughts about causing harm, they aren't me. It just feels like my brain is hijacked by this overwhelming sense of responsibility, and I can't shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are in line with your own values and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean, they're definitely not my values. I ended up using a wire coat hanger to try and fish it out, and I pulled out, like, three cat toys. Which is weird, because I haven't had a cat since my old tabby, Barnaby, passed away five years ago. He was such a good cat, used to sit on my keyboard while I was trying to code... \n\nBut wait, what was the question? Oh, right, the activities. Yeah, I'm just not doing any of the fun stuff anymore. And it's not even just the fun stuff, it's work, too. Like I said before, I missed that major cloud migration deadline recently because I just couldn't bring myself to sign off on the architectural decisions. I was just totally paralyzed by the need to check everything again. Now my team has just started working around me entirely instead of relying on me. So, yeah, it's definitely interfering with pretty much everything I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to your usual way of thinking?\n\nPatient: It's... I mean, it's totally foreign to how I usually am. What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. And even though part of me knows it’s irrational, it hits me like I’m personally responsible and I can’t just move on. It's not me. I used to be the guy who could make quick decisions, you know? \n\nLike when I decided to buy my first motorcycle. I didn't even think about it for more than a day. It was a vintage Honda CB750, beautiful bike. I bought it from this guy out in Long Island who had restored it himself. He had this massive garage, totally tricked out with custom tool chests and neon signs. He actually gave me this old neon Budweiser sign, which I hung up in my basement until the transformer blew and it started buzzing so loudly I couldn't hear the TV. I tried to fix the transformer myself, watching all these YouTube tutorials. There's this one channel, this guy in Australia who fixes old electronics, and he has the most soothing voice. I ended up falling asleep watching a video about repairing a 1980s Betamax player. My uncle had a Betamax player when I was growing up. We used to watch the original Star Wars on it, over and over...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The thoughts. Right. Yeah, so these thoughts about causing harm, they aren't me. It just feels like my brain is hijacked by this overwhelming sense of responsibility, and I can't shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are in line with your own values and beliefs, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean, they're definitely not my values. Like I was just saying, what brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything. And even though part of me knows it’s irrational, it hits me like I’m personally responsible and I can’t just move on. It feels totally outside of who I am. \n\nIt's like... it's like when you're driving and you get a flat tire, right? You didn't plan for it, it's just this sudden, awful thing that derails your whole trip. I had a blowout once on the interstate, driving down to Florida for a tech conference. It was a rental car, a silver sedan, and the spare tire in the trunk was one of those tiny donuts. Have you ever tried driving on the highway with a donut tire? You're not supposed to go over fifty miles an hour, and all these massive semi-trucks are just flying past you, shaking the whole car. I ended up pulling off at this weird little exit in Georgia. There was a diner there that looked like it hadn't been updated since 1975—orange vinyl booths, a jukebox that only played country music. I had the best slice of cherry pie there, though. The waitress, her name tag said 'Dot,' she told me her grandson was learning to code, and she asked me all these questions about Python... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Uh, what I mean is, the thoughts don't feel like *me*. They feel like this heavy, terrifying thing that just... happened to me, and now I'm stuck with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing settings and causing harm are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to your usual way of thinking?\n\nPatient: I think we... wait, yeah, I feel like I just touched on this, but no, it is completely foreign to me. That's exactly why I'm sitting here talking to you. Because normally, I'm a very logical guy, but now I'm just bombarded with these thoughts that if I overlook a single access rule, or ignore one minor warning sign on the security dashboard, the whole system will be compromised and people will lose everything, or someone will actually get hurt. And logically, a part of my brain knows that's a massive, irrational leap, but it just hits me in the gut like I'm entirely, personally responsible for preventing a catastrophe, and I just get stuck there. I can't move on.\n\nIt’s like my brain is glitching. Speaking of glitches, we had this server issue back in 2018. Well, not a full outage, but it felt huge at the time. One of the junior developers pushed a bad update right before the weekend. I was actually at a wedding when my phone started blowing up with alerts. It was my cousin Sarah's wedding. Beautiful venue, this old converted barn up in Vermont. They had these vintage string lights draped everywhere, and a live bluegrass band. I'm not usually a huge bluegrass fan, but they had this banjo player who was just phenomenal. He did this twenty-minute solo that blew everyone away. I actually bought their CD from a little merch table they set up near the open bar. I still have it in the center console of my car, which is stupid because I don't even have a CD player in my new car. I finally caved and bought an EV last year, so it's all just one giant touchscreen. Sometimes the screen gets these awful fingerprint smudges on it, and I have to keep a special microfiber cloth in the door pocket...\n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I lost the thread.
